Our <term>Fire</term> <note>original symbol: Δ; used here to represent the primary active principle or internal heat</note> is as if lukewarm, and it is increased by cold; see folio 90. <term>Water</term> <note>original symbol: ▽</note>, through cold...

**Note Well:** The Weight of Nature, page 91, bottom of the page.

The effect of "active darkness" <note>original: "Tenebrarum activarum"</note>—according to Wintring <note>likely a reference to a specific author or the process of "wintering" and cooling</note>—in nature is the mother of cold; it is the mass of collection and the beginning of all cold. Just as light is the principle and source of Heat.

Philaletha <note>Referring to Eirenaeus Philalethes, a famous 17th-century alchemist whose works were widely studied in the 1700s</note>, folio 54 and 55: The face of cold is below in the depths, while the **Divine Darkness** <note>original: "Caligo Divina"; a mystical concept of a darkness that is "too bright" for human senses, often associated with the presence of the Creator</note> is above the comprehension of the <unclear>unlearned</unclear>. Between these two lies nature. **Moisture** is the passive element; for the slightest **Heat** changes water into smoke <note>meaning vapor or steam</note>, and the slightest cold turns that same smoke back into water again.

# Editor’s Preface.

Kind reader, I hereby present to you a small treatise, <note>German: "Tractätlein"</note> about which I have nothing further to say (for the work praises the master itself) other than that it is one of the most sincere and useful. And since this author not only mentions the **Eternal Wisdom** <note>In Hermetic thought, "Eternal Wisdom" often refers to *Sophia*, the divine feminine aspect of God or the spiritual insight required to understand nature's secrets.</note> once within it, but also mentions seeking and practicing it here and there with brief words, I also recalled that I have a short discussion of it in one of my own books. I have therefore wished to include it here for a better understanding of the author, alongside three small treatises by **Theophrastus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim, known as Paracelsus (1493–1541), a foundational figure in toxicology and occult chemistry.</note> that have become very rare. Of one of these, the excellent **Sendivogius** <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a renowned Polish alchemist and physician credited with discovering oxygen long before Priestley, though he called it the "food of life" in the air.</note> himself speaks thus in his second epistle:

> **Paracelsus**, whose writings are divine lights; yet, if you can find a certain supplement <note>original: "Codicill," referring to a small instructional book or appendix.</note> called the ***Chemical Psalter, or Manual of Paracelsus*** <note>original: "Psalterium Chymicum seu Manuale Paracelsi."</note>, you will [discover] all the secrets of the most hid- <meta>The text cuts off mid-word due to the catchword "ver-," likely "verborgenen" (hidden/secret).</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the editor introduces a collection of rare alchemical texts, justifying their value by citing the endorsements of legendary figures like Paracelsus and Michael Sendivogius. He emphasizes the practical and spiritual utility of the works, specifically mentioning the "Eternal Wisdom" and the "Chemical Psalter."</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, Michael Sendivogius, Eternal Wisdom, Chemical Psalter, alchemy, Hermeticism, preface, book collecting, occult secrets</keywords>

...hidden **Cabalæ** <term>Cabala|In this Hermetic context, it refers to "Christian Kabbalah," a system used by Renaissance and Baroque scholars to find mystical and alchemical meanings in the structure of creation.</term> and the physically proven secrets, and see the whole doctrine of the alchemical science therein with open eyes. Furthermore, Paracelsus’s *Treatise on the Physical Tincture* <note>original Latin: "Tractatus de Tinctura Physica"</note> is not to be neglected. To these two I have also added the third, because the author refers to it in the latter, which he named the *Apocalypse of Hermes* <note>original Latin: "Apocalipsin Hermetis"</note>. I have borrowed the aforementioned small treatises from the Imperial and Royal Library <note>German: "Kayſerl. Königl. Bibliothek," referring to the court library in Vienna.</note> mostly retaining his own words. I did want to omit or even change some things in his preface and elsewhere, since he defends himself so crudely against the **Physicians** <term>Medicos|Specifically the academic doctors of Paracelsus's time, whom he famously criticized for relying on ancient texts like Galen rather than practical chemistry.</term> (who were in conflict with him at the time); however, since he mixed in various secrets here and there throughout his defense, I preferred to leave it just as he wrote it, all the more because it does not concern anyone living now, and in our own time, medicine is generally in a different state. Fare thee well, and use all five for your benefit, which I hereby wish you from the bottom of my heart.

# Preface.

I once was annoyed myself, upon beginning this study, by the multitude and simultaneous obscurity of the **Hermetic authors** <term>Hermetic authors|Writers focused on alchemy, occult philosophy, and the teachings traditionally attributed to the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus.</term>, so I did not wish to increase this number with yet another obscure book. No: that is not my intention; nor is it to make myself known to those who possess this great secret through profound and otherwise impossible-to-grasp sentences, parables, enigmas, and hieroglyphs; for I can well sit quietly in silence and mind my own business. Rather, after I considered that among an extraordinarily large number of authors, while each has done his best to explain the **Philosopher's Stone** <term>Philosopher's Stone|The "Lapis Philosophorum," the central goal of alchemy, believed to transmute base metals into gold and serve as a universal medicine for the body and soul.</term> in such a way that it remained just as hidden as before—despite the truth being preserved therein, with the exception of a few of the more modern writers—not a single one has properly shown the **Students of Hermes** <term>Students of Hermes|A traditional name for practitioners of alchemy, also known as "Sons of Art."</term> the way and method alongside the requirements for it, nor treated it extensively according to the nature of the matter; and at the same time, what this so great a work and secret entails and wishes to say of itself, whereby many

<meta>catchword: short-</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the author expresses his frustration with the intentional obscurity of previous alchemical writers and declares his intention to provide a clearer, more practical guide for students of the craft.</summary>
<keywords>Hermetic authors, Philosopher's Stone, Students of Hermes, alchemical education, clarity in writing, parables and enigmas</keywords>

# Preface.

<meta>catchword: Therefore</meta>

...short-sighted and weak minds <note>The original German *blöde* here signifies a lack of mental clarity or physical "dimness" rather than modern "stupidity."</note> would be held back, and right from the start be discouraged by this heavenly shimmer and light from escaping their misfortune and ruin. Thus, they might apply their time and money better than having to learn the truth only after many years and much labor, alongside the ruin of their bodies and means. This has brought some to the point where, instead of finding the **Philosopher's Stone** <term>Philosopher's Stone|The *Lapis Philosophorum*, the ultimate goal of alchemy, believed to possess the power of transmutation and universal healing.</term>, they were forced to eat common stones for lack of bread. <note>The author is making a grim pun: by chasing the "Philosopher's Stone," these failed alchemists became so poor they had nothing to eat but literal stones from the street.</note> And this is my intention to do all the more—since I could not write anything more in the main work itself than others before me have already written—so that I might all the more perfectly treat the nature of this **Art** <term>Art|Often capitalized in these texts to refer specifically to Alchemy, the "Hermetic Art."</term>, the required sciences, necessary circumstances, equipment, opportunity, time, the manner of reading philosophical books, and the like. In these matters, the reader will learn and discover just as much, and even more, than if I had occupied myself extensively with the preparation of the Stone alone. For by doing so, as if providing a **master key**, I demonstrate and instruct how to comprehend the subject in general. My goal is to bring those chosen for this work to it all the sooner, and to lead the others away from it—for the benefit and salvation of both.

<summary>The author explains that his preface acts as a "master key" to alchemy, intended to guide the worthy while deterring those who would only find financial and physical ruin. He argues that understanding the practical requirements and how to interpret alchemical texts is more valuable than repeating standard recipes for the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, master key, Hermetic Art, alchemical equipment, financial ruin, education, interpretation of texts</keywords>

# Preface.

<meta>catchword: Content</meta>

Therefore, I have titled this little treatise the **Hermetic North Star**, (for since in this day and age, through the writings of the philosophers and their manifold described works and paths, it has come to pass that a lover and seeker of this **Art** <term>Art|Alchemy, specifically the "Hermetic Art" or the Great Work of transmutation.</term> finds himself just like a mariner <note>Original: *Schiffsfender*. While modern German uses this for a ship's bumper, here it refers to a navigator or sailor struggling against the elements.</note> upon the vast world-ocean with an incorrect compass, where he must orient himself more by the **North Star** than anything else) to light your ways, and finally to lead you into the fortunate harbor to obtain the **Golden Fleece** <term>Golden Fleece|A mythological symbol frequently used by alchemists to represent the final, successful completion of the Philosopher's Stone.</term>.

Therefore, brace your sails well, and set them properly according to the wind that comes from the sunrise <note>Original: *Aufgang*. East.</note> and midday <note>Original: *Mittag*. South.</note>, and leads you toward evening <note>Original: *Abend*. West.</note>; then turn your rigging again, catch the wind from the west, and travel toward morning <note>Original: *Morgen*. East.</note>, and you will arrive in the desired harbor, if you will meanwhile contemplate my **North Star** in the twilight, from whence you can then set out into all lands, richly laden. Thanking God first for this, then helping the poor, and generally assisting all your brothers and neighbors as much as you ever have the opportunity; but above all, do not forget to use such a treasure for the salvation of your soul <note>The author emphasizes that alchemical wealth is not for greed, but for Christian charity and spiritual growth.</note>.

<summary>The author introduces the title of his work, the "Hermetic North Star," using a nautical metaphor to describe how his guide will lead the alchemist through the "ocean" of confusing literature to the "Golden Fleece." He reminds the reader that the ultimate goal of the Art is charity toward the poor and the salvation of one's soul.</summary>

<keywords>Hermetic North Star, Golden Fleece, nautical metaphor, charity, salvation of the soul, Alchemy instructions</keywords>

## First Chapter.

### What the Hermetic Mastery is, by how many names it is called, and why?

The Hermetic Mastery <term>Hermetic Mastery|The ultimate achievement in alchemy, often called the "Great Work," which involves the creation of the Philosopher's Stone and the attainment of universal knowledge.</term> is that greatest of all arts and sciences which encompasses all other sciences of the seven liberal arts <term>Seven Liberal Arts|The traditional curriculum of medieval and Renaissance education, consisting of the trivium (grammar, logic, rhetoric) and the quadrivium (arithmetic, geometry, music, astronomy).</term> within itself; it is, as it were, their mother from whom they were born. Whoever possesses it has the most perfect knowledge of the three kingdoms of the world <term>Three Kingdoms|The division of nature into the mineral, vegetable, and animal realms. Alchemists believed the same life-force or "spirit" animated all three.</term>. For this reason, Hermes—as the forefather of all lovers <note>In alchemical texts, "lovers" (Liebhaber) or "sons of art" refers to dedicated practitioners and students of alchemy.</note> of this art, which he left behind for us inscribed upon an emerald tablet <note>original: "smaragdenen Stein"; referring to the *Tabula Smaragdina*, a legendary cryptic text said to contain the secrets of the universe.</note> so that it might not remain hidden from posterity, and about which he wrote the best, albeit the shortest, account—was named Trismegistus <note>original: "Trismegistus"; meaning "Thrice-Greatest." This title traditionally signifies his mastery over the three parts of the wisdom of the whole universe: alchemy, astrology, and theurgy.</note>. And since the Creator cannot be better recognized than through His creation, it follows that he who [understands] the three kingdoms of the world the

<summary>This introductory chapter defines alchemy (Hermetic Mastery) as the "mother" of all sciences and introduces Hermes Trismegistus as the legendary founder who recorded the art's secrets on the Emerald Tablet.</summary>
<keywords>Hermetic Mastery, Seven Liberal Arts, Three Kingdoms of Nature, Hermes Trismegistus, Emerald Tablet, alchemy, divine creation</keywords>

...since at that time their simple and quiet way of life was very helpful to them in attaining this great **mystery** and **art**, by means of which they then served others with counsel and deed. It was observed that among them all, there was not a single person as wise or as learned as these men; consequently, everyone came before them in disputes and other occurrences, as if to a judge or a chief, to receive their verdict and decision. 

And since, in addition, such wise men instructed and assisted them in other matters—both regarding the maintenance of the body through the improvement of clothing and food, and comfort in the construction of dwellings and the like—the people regarded such a man as their foremost, most noble head, or the principal person among them. Because he surpassed all others in wisdom, goodness, and helpfulness, they designated <note>original: "exprimiret"</note> him with the name of **King**. They showed him honors and goodwill in everything he advised them, and thus lived as happily and voluntarily under him as they would under their own father, benefactor, and king.

As is known of the **Knights** <note>Likely referring to the Knights Templar or the legendary origins of the Rosicrucians</note>, and can still be seen in the institutions of those today. | **Solomon** | The biblical king traditionally viewed by alchemists as the ultimate master of the Philosopher's Stone. |
| **Moses** | The biblical prophet, often cited in alchemy for his ability to "burn" and "grind" the Golden Calf into a drinkable powder. |

# First Chapter.

...not only sees and distinguishes things with the outer sight and understanding—such as, this is a type of mineral, that a metal, this a quartz, that a rock, this a vegetable <note>original: "Vegetabile," here meaning any plant matter.</note>, of plants, shrubs or trees; this is a sandy earth, that a mother-earth, clay <note>original: "Lätten," a term for a fatty, plastic clay.</note>, bole <note>original: "Bolus," a fine, earthy clay used in medicine and as a pigment.</note> and the like—but rather with inner sight and understanding; as: this mineral’s quality is more saline, possessing much of the Mercurial <note>In alchemy, "Mercurial" refers to the principle of fluidity, volatility, and spirit, one of the three primary substances (Tria Prima).</note>, but little of the Sulphurous. This vegetable has little fixed salt, little sulfurous oiliness, but more of the watery or Mercurial part. O folly! which cannot be greater!

# First Chapter. What the Hermetic [Art] Is, etc.

...[they] do not tell you plainly how to make the philosopher's stone <term>Philosopher’s Stone: The legendary substance (the "Universal") capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the Elixir of Life.</term>, but they say more than clearly that no particular <term>Particular: In alchemy, a "particular" refers to a specific, limited result—such as a minor medicinal cure or a partial transmutation—as opposed to the "Universal" power of the Stone itself.</term> exists without the universal; yet you wish to be wiser than they and not believe them, and would rather have a branch before the trunk. Indeed, it is through just such a swarm <note>Referring to the "dissolute rabble" of unqualified seekers mentioned on the previous page.</note> that this noble art has fallen into contempt and discredit in the world; for after they saw that they had been deceived by their own stupidity, many of them did not shrink from deceiving others as well, or at the very least from reviling such an art both secretly and publicly. But was it not also one of the causes—that such fellows undertook to learn this greatest of sciences—because none of the philosophers has rightly brought to light the majesty, greatness, understanding, deep science, and weighty essence of this art? And am I not right to prove this at length, and indeed from its first origin, in order to stem this cause?

Therefore I say in sum and as a conclusion to this chapter—which you ought to consider well—that you should take to heart and reflect upon the greatness and importance of this work, which I have dealt with extensively enough. You must also measure your own conduct and capability accordingly, and test well whether you believe yourself to be equal to it or not; which you shall hear further of in the following chapter.

## The Second Chapter.

### What this Work requires of an Artist, and how he ought to be constituted?

From the circumstances cited above, it can already be partly inferred what kind of person this great art <term>Art: In this context, "Art" refers to Alchemy or the "Great Work" (Ars Magna), which was seen as a combination of laboratory science and spiritual discipline.</term> requires, and how he must be constituted in order to attain it; I wish, therefore, to summarize this briefly and add what is still lacking. Draw therefore a comparison between the art and the artist <term>Artist: The "Artist" (Künstler) is the alchemist or practitioner of the Hermetic science.</term>, and the conclusion will follow of its own accord.

I say then, firstly: it is a great secret; thus the artist must also be secret and discreet. In truth, this point is not so trivial as many imagine—some even believe it is wrong to keep it silent and not reveal it because it is a "good thing." However, they do not consider the consequences, which could be nothing other than highly harmful. For the better a thing is, and the greater it is, the more harmful it also becomes when it is misused. 

A medicine, which is in itself good and potentially useful to the sick, becomes harmful if it is taken all at once and in great quantity when it should only have been administered over time and in stages; and the greater and stronger such a medicine is, the more dangerous it becomes. Just as with the medicines <note>original: "Medicamenta"</note> prepared from mercury <note>original: "Mercurio"</note>,

<summary>The author begins the second chapter by arguing that the character of the alchemist must mirror the nature of the art itself, specifically emphasizing that a "secret" art requires a "discreet" practitioner. He warns that revealing alchemical secrets would be as dangerous as administering a powerful medicine in an incorrect, excessive dosage.</summary>

<keywords>alchemist, secrecy, discretion, medical ethics, mercury, misuse of knowledge, Hermetic Art</keywords>

But the best thing of all is that God always keeps His hand over this **great mystery** and **science**, and will never permit such a thing. For there is not just one example, but many, showing that not only those who discovered it fully, but even those who revealed more than was proper, were punished immediately thereafter; you may certainly believe these accounts cited by the philosophers, for they are no mere fairy tales. Fourthly, it is most commonly called the **Philosophical Art**, or the **Philosopher's Stone**; consequently, you too must be **Philosophers**, or at least devoted to philosophy, which you will see for yourselves is necessary so that you can weigh and undertake everything with true understanding. By this, I mean the true philosophy, which searches through everything with the greatest subtlety, sees into the very innermost part, and perceives or handles nothing for which it cannot provide the true cause. To distinguish it from common philosophy, I will, for the sake of brevity, content myself with citing a few examples:

Common philosophy generally establishes four elements: **Water**, **Earth**, **Air**, and **Fire**. True philosophy, however, holds that there are no simple elements <note>original: "Elementa simplicia." In classical physics, these were the building blocks of matter.</note>; for Water also contains Fire, Air, and Earth within itself; Earth contains Fire, Air, and Water; and so it is with the others; rather, they are composed elements <note>original: "Elementa Elementata." This hermetic concept suggests that what we see as "Water" is actually a mixture of all four elemental forces.</note>.

## The Second Chapter

...forth fruit after its kind. True philosophy sees that this seed, which is sown into the earth, is not the true seed itself—for that is invisible—but rather only the casing or the body. Inside its center, like a subtle little point, resides the true seed as a subtle, invisible spirit, much like the soul within the human body. When this little grain is sown into the earth as its mother, it does not rot; instead, through the moisture and the **central fires** <term>Central Fires: In alchemical thought, an internal heat source within the Earth that matures minerals and sparks the growth of plants.</term> within the earth, this spirit or seed inside the grain is set into motion. It then unfolds itself, draws its own likeness to itself as **nutriment** <term>Nutriment: The specific "food" or elemental substance that a plant pulls from its environment to build its physical form.</term>, and begins to sprout, turn green, and grow. 

This can be clearly seen from the following: if one places a seed grain on a stove or another warm place until it becomes hot, and then tries to sow it, it would never produce fruit throughout all eternity. This is because, although the grain appears to the eye to have its previous weight and shape, the spirit or seed has flown away or been driven out by the heat, leaving the grain behind as a dead body.

# What this Work Means for an Artist, etc.

...[say it] is already the perfect **seed** <note>original: "Saamen"</note> in the form of little **animalcules** <term>Animalcules: From the Latin 'animalculum', meaning 'little animal.' Early microscopists used this term for microscopic organisms, including sperm cells, which some believed were fully-formed miniature humans.</term>, which can also be seen moving in moisture through **microscopes** <note>original: "Microscopia"</note>. They say that after cohabitation by both parties, one or more of these crawl into an **ovule** <note>original: "Ovulum," meaning a little egg</note> of the **ovary** <note>original: "Eyerstock"</note>, and thus the mother becomes a participant of one or two fruits <note>In this context, "fruits" refers to developing embryos or children.</note>. For even if I temporarily accept the seed as they describe it, I ask: why couldn't four, five, or even more such creatures crawl in at once, just like one or two? And if, perhaps, two ovules—one from each **ovary** <note>original: "Ovario"</note>—should move **toward the uterus** <note>original: "ad uterum"</note>, and so many animalcules then got inside, what a misfortune it would be for the woman if the poor soul had to give birth to a whole dozen children! Truly: they would be much to be pitied, and yet in such a manner, this would have to happen very often. 

I also ask: do these animalcules crawl through the membranes into the ovule? Or if not, who shows them the way so that they enter through the tiny opening of the ovule's stem, which later becomes the **umbilical cord** <note>original: "Corda umbilicalis"</note>? Besides the fact that all the other animalcules would then be lost and created in vain! No: God the wise **Creator** <note>original: "Schöpfer"</note> creates nothing in vain, nor is His design so wasteful.

**True Philosophy** therefore recognizes that the seed of the male consists not of animalcules, but of a viscous substance, [composed] of the most subtle

# The Second Chapter

The same applies in the mineral kingdom; there, much is debated regarding the **generation of metals** <note>The alchemical process by which metals are thought to "grow" or form within the earth</note>: some say it happens through sulfur and mercury, others through sulfur and arsenic, others through vitriol and the like. True understanding recognizes that the true generation of metals does not proceed from any of these, as all such substances were generated by the same source as the metals themselves. Rather, amidst such confused matters and various opinions, it goes back to the very first beginning, and indeed to the **creation of the world** itself; <margin>Genesis 1</margin> there it finds that in the **Chaos**, where everything lay confusedly mixed together, the **Spirit of God**, which hovered over the waters, began to move and separate the waters, driving the more subtle to the heavens above and the coarser to the bottom; from which the earth then appeared, as did the great lights from the upper part, which then, acting further upon each other, brought everything in the **three kingdoms** <note>The three traditional divisions of nature: Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral</note> to perfection, a process which continues even to this hour.

[...pores of the earth] produces and matures various minerals and metals <note>original: "Mineralia und Metalla"</note>, depending on whether it encounters a pure matrix <note>original: "Matricem"; in alchemy, the "womb" or formative environment within the earth where minerals grow</note>. # The Second Chapter

These matters will mostly concern the mystical and the sacred, as we can see in our contemplation of Holy Scripture <note>original: "Heil. Schrift"</note> in general. For this reason, they may rightly be called supernatural or beyond reason <note>original: "übervernünftig"</note>, to distinguish them from common things. This is done so that not everyone might be so bold as to immediately grasp and interpret such matters with their own worldly understanding, and subsequently bring them forth in a false and erroneous way, which causes no small number of mistakes and errors to arise in the world. This is especially true if they are put forward by a man who has already made himself famous in the world through other sciences and has gained a following. This can be proven from Holy Scripture alone, where from a single book, which contains only one doctrine or faith, over three hundred doctrines and faiths have arisen.

And to illustrate this to you with just one example of how even learned people mostly fail in this regard: Mr. Hasselquist <note>Fredrik Hasselquist (1722–1752) was a student of the famous botanist Carl Linnaeus. He traveled to Egypt and the Levant to document natural history; his journals were published posthumously as *Iter Palaestinum*</note>, in his travelogue and investigation of the lands of Egypt, Syria, and their production of various insects, plants, and other rarities, has—among other things—asserted through false delusion and shallow understanding that the Manna <note>The miraculous food provided by God to the Israelites during their travels in the desert (Exodus 16)</note>, which the Israelites ate in the wilderness by Mount Sinai and which is said to have fallen from heaven, may be nothing other than that which is commonly known to occur here—namely, that here and there in the air a moist-

->What this work offers an artist, etc.<-

...moisture gathered by means of the elements, thickened, and fell down. Oh, what a foolish concept! I would not bet much against the possibility that the author mistook it for so-called star-jelly <note>original: "Stern-Putzen"; a gelatinous substance found on the ground, historically believed to be the remains of falling stars, but now known to be a type of cyanobacteria called Nostoc</note>, which likewise occurs in this country. Had he read the text of Exodus 16:14 correctly and considered it well, where it says: "And when the dew had fallen and covered the whole earth, behold, there lay something in the wilderness—note—thin and small, even as a small grain that has been husked in a mortar-stone, like the frost on the land." 

Since this "something" must therefore have been dry, and not purely moist or fluid—to which the name of a grain <term>Körnlein: a small, hard seed or pellet</term> can be attributed, and its husking or cleaning in a hard instrument, such as a mortar, can be described—he would have done better to keep his thoughts from being made public until he had devised something better, or perhaps left it alone entirely. This is even more true had he compared the other text, Numbers 11:7–8, with the previous one, where it says: "But the Manna was like coriander seed, and looked like bdellium <note>original: "Bdellion"; a semi-transparent oleo-gum resin extracted from trees, similar to myrrh</note>, and the people ran back and forth and gathered it, and crushed it with mills and ground it in mortars, and boiled it in pots, and made cakes from it, and it had a taste like oil-cakes." Now, I wish the author had hit upon the idea of testing this himself with his own material: what a strange bit of cooking he would have accomplished!

# The Second Chapter.

...e, in order to produce a cake, and moreover with the taste of oil; not to mention why it is frequently reported—as also in verse 9—that the dew fell first, and only afterward the Manna fell upon it? This is just as with people of different sizes, where the garment of the large person is no larger than that of the small one, when considered according to their person. Why (v. 20), when some people left a portion of it over, did it become rotten by the next morning and have worms growing in it? Also (v. 21), why did it melt from the sun when it grew hot?

Could such things happen if the aforementioned Manna were what the author claims? Or as others believe, who take it for a resin <term>Gummi: a hardened plant sap or resin found on trees</term> from the trees—which would much rather be thickened by the sun—to say nothing of the fact that it is compared to the Word (according to Deuteronomy 8:3) which proceeds from the mouth of the Lord.

Furthermore, he mentions the locusts that John the Baptist ate. He claims that large locusts grow around the Jordan, which the people there are accustomed to eating out of poverty, as well as the customs of the land. I know for certain that Mr. Hasselquist <note>Fredrik Hasselquist (1722–1752) was a student of Carl Linnaeus who traveled to the Eastern Mediterranean to provide botanical evidence for biblical accounts</note> did not see a single person eat such animals, any more than he himself would have eaten them in the greatest famine; and the people would sooner take refuge in herbs and roots...

## The Second Chapter

<meta>catchword: path</meta>

I cannot do otherwise but confess that it would not be proper for me, and would even be very wrong, to state flatly what the Holy Scripture, or the Divine Spirit through Moses, did not undertake to do. In this, all **Hermetic Philosophers** <term>Philosophi Hermetici: practitioners of alchemy and esoteric wisdom who sought the hidden spirit within matter</term>, who are well aware of this, will bear witness for me. Yet, for the sake of true students, let me mention this: I say that the manna, the locusts <note>The author argues here that the "locusts" eaten by John the Baptist were not insects, but a spiritual substance.</note>, and the wild honey that Jonathan ate during the pursuit of the enemies under his father Saul, are all one and the same. The text of **Exodus 16:31** <note>original: "Exod. 16. 31."</note> already points to this latter point: "and it was like coriander seed, white"—or pure—"and the taste of it was like wafers made with honey." It is a **food of angels** <term>Engelspeise: literally "angel-food," referring to the celestial or spiritual nature of Manna</term>, as the Scripture often reports, and is consequently full of power, light, and the Divine Spirit.

Furthermore, I conclude that it is most unjust to present such mysteries, as well as others, in histories and geographic descriptions so dismissively and falsely—things which the unlearned and inquisitive people of today's corrupt world endorse with extraordinary applause. They believe they have grasped something wondrous, exalting the author beyond measure for his "ingenious" thoughts, and thus fall into error along with him. I shall remain silent regarding similar matters from other authors and only mention the **Great Flood** <term>Sündfluth: the biblical Deluge</term>: where some, such as **Mr. Maupertuis** <note>Pierre Louis Maupertuis (1698–1759), a French mathematician and philosopher who proposed a scientific theory that a comet passing close to Earth caused the Great Flood by pulling water from the oceans or through its tail.</note>, claim that an errant celestial body or comet, which through its orbital path

## Chapter Three

### Requirements and Means, along with Time and Opportunity to accomplish them.

<margin>1.</margin>Among the requirements and other means of this **Great Art** <term>Great Art: an alchemical term (Ars Magna) referring to the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</term>, one of the most important is that a person be his own master, or if not, at least be able to have the time required for it at his disposal. Otherwise, you will produce nothing in this world, and it is better to let the work wait until you find such time. # Requirements and Means, along with Time, etc.

...family, you cannot devote all your time to it, and consequently either this art or your household must suffer harm: so you do well, in order to gain all the time needed, to temporarily hand over your affairs to a friend to manage, or to lease your property to someone for a fixed sum for a few years. In this way, if you should fail in certain points during the first year, you may be more fortunate in the second and achieve success.

Besides time, however, you must also have the proper **opportunity**, otherwise the former is of no use to you without the latter. For just as you must have time to make the **Stone** <term>Stone: the Philosopher's Stone (Lapis Philosophorum), the legendary goal of alchemy capable of transmutation and healing</term> yourself, because you can entrust it to no one else, so too must you have such an opportunity that you do not betray anything to others through your work; this is not as small an obstacle as many might imagine, and is much harder than the first. But I know for certain that there must have been many among the **Adepts** <term>Adepts: individuals who have successfully mastered the secrets of alchemy and achieved the Great Work</term> whom this circumstance compelled, as it did me, to postpone their work for several years. 

I myself have unfortunately experienced this latter circumstance: for since I was so fortunate that through diligent **prayer** <note>original: "Gebeth"</note>, hope, and trust in God, and persistent **study** <note>original: "Studium"</note>, I finally glimpsed the light all at once, which showed me my error, so that I had to immediately put a stop to my **laboring** <term>Laboring: the active, physical practice of alchemical experimentation</term>, dismantle my **furnaces** <note>original: "Oefen"</note>, and have my many **glasses** <note>original: "Gläser"</note> and **vessels** <note>original: "Geschirre"</note> carried up to the attic, because I then clearly recognized how it was possible, and tha-

### Chapter Three

...to find it quite easy to perform all operations—such as **calcining** <term>calcining: heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to reduce it to a fine powder</term>, **distilling** <term>distilling: the process of heating a liquid to create vapor and then cooling it to collect the purified substance</term>, **reverberating** <term>reverberating: heating material in a furnace where the flame is reflected or "reverberated" off the roof onto the substance</term>, **imbibing** <term>imbibing: the gradual addition of liquid to a solid until it is fully saturated</term>, or **dissolving** and **coagulating** <term>dissolving and coagulating: the essential alchemical cycle of turning a solid into a liquid (solution) and back into a solid (congealing)</term>, which amount to the same thing—in a single furnace with one fire and in one vessel. The author suggests he performed the work many times in his mind through visualization and theory while waiting for the physical opportunity.</note> often and many times in my head. I believed it impossible to be certain of these details except through the physical labor itself.

I realized that there is actually only one way, with the difference that the so-called **wet path** is shorter, faster, nobler, and easier; for instead of the long and tedious purification of the earths and their subsequent separation, one extracts the noblest essence through its **alcohol** <term>alcohol: here referring to a highly refined spirit or "quintessence" used as a solvent for extraction, rather than modern drinking alcohol</term> and then **coagulates** it further, just as in the dry path.

The best thing, therefore, if someone does not have their own house, is that he takes up a summer building or a small garden house in the country or in a suburb. It does not need to be large; an above-ground and an underground vault with a pair of windows facing **east** and **west** <note>original: "Auf- und Niedergang"</note> is quite enough; the most important thing is that you are alone and can keep your things safe. But however small it is, it still causes some expenses for self-maintenance, so that you may recognize from this—even if the **material** <term>materia: the raw substance or "First Matter" used in the alchemical process</term> costs nothing and the **instruments** <term>instrumenta: the specialized tools, vessels, and apparatuses of the chemist</term> cost little—that no poor or needy person can do it.

In conclusion, I repeat this chapter together with the previous one, and say that a **student of Hermes** <term>Hermetis-Schüler: a practitioner of alchemy, named after the legendary founder Hermes Trismegistus</term>, besides the qualities mentioned in the previous chapter, must also have time, opportunity, and means, if he wishes to obtain **mastery** <term>Meisterschaft: the successful completion of the Great Work or the creation of the Philosopher's Stone</term>. Fortunate, therefore, and indeed fortunate is that man and lover of this art in whom all the above-mentioned circumstances are found! He is truly to be congratulated from the heart. But how few are they, and how...

### Chapter Three.
<meta>catchword: only</meta>

how small is the number among whom all of this is found; therefore, it is not to be wondered at that so few are found who attain this art and possess the **philosopher’s stone** <term>philosophischen Stein: the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and acting as a universal medicine</term>, so that out of sheer rarity, the world will hardly believe that this stone truly exists. Still less so, if one adds to the above the divine election and the **right matter** <term>materia: the specific starting substance required for the alchemical process</term>; for even if he has the right matter, he still cannot recognize the **inner spirit** <term>Geist: the vital, hidden essence within the physical substance</term>—how it is constituted—until the very end. For you can well see a person, but you cannot see his soul; in the dark, you indeed see the lantern, but you do not see the light shining within it, whether it be a common tallow light or a wax candle.

So too, the **artist** <term>Künstler: the alchemist or practitioner of the Hermetic arts</term> has everything before him: **earth, water, spirit, oil, salt** and the like; but only at the very end does he recognize whether he has the **blessing** <note>original: "Seegen," referring to both the success of the chemical reaction and divine favor</note> or not. And when one considers this, one’s courage fails completely. Truly! If beginners could see this, if they could have an eye so sharp that they could distinguish and recognize every other glance, and if they could believe all these truths that I have presented, then certainly hardly one out of a thousand **devotees** <note>original: "Liebhabern," meaning those who love the art or amateurs</note> would dare to undertake the work, and they would be right to do so. Therefore, as a final conclusion to this chapter, I say: let he who finds himself in such doubt pray to God that He may enlighten him; but not

## Chapter Four.

### On the Reading of Philosophical-Hermetic Books.

Dear children and **disciples of Hermes** <term>discipel Hermetis: followers of the teachings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy</term>, lovers of the divine mysteries and the **Art** <term>Kunst: the "Great Work" of alchemy, encompassing both chemical experimentation and spiritual transformation</term>, now I begin to speak specifically with you. Previously, I have spoken generally, at length, clearly, and truthfully to everyone, so that those to whom fortune and nature have not granted the aforementioned gifts, abilities, and means may remain behind. Such people should not force their way into the **sanctuary** <term>Heiligthum: the sacred, hidden knowledge of the alchemical secrets</term> by force, for the door will not be opened to them anyway. Thus, they can apply their few **natural gifts** <term>Natur-Gaben: innate talents or intellectual capacities</term> more usefully to lesser things according to their nature, whereas they would only suffer harm from these higher matters.

# Chapter Four.

But you, dear children, you can now proceed further with me; I will also faithfully lead you further like a father, with the same sincerity and faithfulness that I have already begun in previous chapters and will continue in those to follow. In these, I have revealed many hidden truths to you which—you may believe me on my honor—no **philosopher** <term>Philosophus: an alchemist or natural philosopher</term> has dared to express so clearly. Even I would not and could not have placed them in their proper location without breaking the **secrecy** <term>Verschwiegenheit: the traditional vow of silence or discretion regarding alchemical secrets</term> that I have so highly praised to you. But in you, I have this confidence: that you will not only perceive it with your sharp understanding and inner eyes but will also know how to faithfully preserve it within your hearts.

Now, concerning the reading of the **books of the philosophers** <term>Philosophorum Bücher: the established canon of hermetic and alchemical writings</term>, I tell you generally in advance: once you have chosen the authentic ones—those which are cited and referenced by the authors themselves—you should grant them complete faith and trust. However, all this must be understood within the **possibility of nature** <term>Natur Möglichkeit: the boundaries of what is physically possible according to natural law; the idea that alchemy perfects nature but does not act against it</term>, and not like others who, instead of learning from them, rather seek to **correct** <note>corrigiren</note> them and claim to know better. This is evident when such people praise the authors on one page and scold them on another; then they say: "Ah! There he has written clearly, there he speaks well; but here, ah, there is nothing, there he holds the handle in his hand <note>original: "da hält er das Heft in der Hand" — an idiom suggesting the author is being intentionally evasive or withholding the core secret</note>, or, that says..."

### Fourth Chapter.

Furthermore, take note that you should read the **philosophers** <term>Philosophen: the master alchemists and authors of Hermetic texts</term> in your mother tongue, or in that language which you understand best and are most proficient in; for you should know that you must not understand them according to the literal letter, but according to the sense of what they wish to suggest to you beneath it. Moreover, once you have reached the point where you grasp their meaning because the **principles** <term>Principia: the fundamental elements, laws, or starting materials of the Great Work</term> are already known to you, they have yet another meaning hidden beneath this sense (much like **Holy Scripture** <note>original: "H. Schrift"</note>), so that with one language they say three things at once, all three of which are correct and true at the same time. For instance: they speak of **gold** <term>Gold: in alchemy, this can refer to common gold, the "philosophical" gold of the wise, or the perfected state of matter</term>, **mercury** <term>Mercurio: the fluid, transformative principle often symbolized by quicksilver</term>, and its growth. In such a manner, they also often write of two **operations** <term>Operationes: specific laboratory procedures or chemical transformations</term> at once. # On the Reading of Philosophical-Hermetic [Works], etc.

...according to the mystical sense, it can be understood: so too can the true **philosophers** <term>Philosophos: the master alchemists and authors of Hermetic texts</term> be grasped no otherwise than through a—note well—truly philosophical understanding. From this, learn how highly necessary it is to read these books in your mother tongue, so that you may penetrate with your own mind right deep into their meaning, in order to pluck the truth from them.

Beside this, you must also read the **philosophers** in the language in which they themselves wrote: for no one, no one I say—let him be as knowledgeable in the language as he will—can translate <note>original: "vertiren," from the Latin *vertere*, meaning to turn or translate</note> a **philosopher** so that he retains his entire meaning, except another true **philosopher** himself; who then either writes himself, or at the very least provides a commentary <note>original: "commentiret"</note>, which latter happens but seldom, though often indeed by **sophists** <term>Sophisten: a derogatory term in alchemy for "puffers" or pretenders who lacked true insight and only followed literal, often incorrect, recipes</term>. Moreover, the **philosophers** have also diligently misspelled one word or another, for no small purpose; as for example, where it stood "by **subtilization**" <term>Subtilisation: the process of making a substance refined, thin, or volatile</term>, another has come along, set it right, and made it "**sublimation**" <term>Sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then solidifies again</term>, and thereby taken away the advantage <note>The author suggests that "errors" in alchemical texts are often intentional blinds to hide secrets from the uninitiated; correcting them actually ruins the riddle.</note>. Likewise, where it stood—do not forget the **Tomb of Hermes** <note>original: "Se. Hermetis," an abbreviation for *Sepulchrum Hermetis*</note>—another believes it to be a printing error, and in the first word, which was not written out, changes the *e* into an *i*, and thus puts *Si*, or even writes it out in full, making the **Seal of Hermes** <note>original: "Sigillum Hermetis"</note> instead of the **Tomb of Hermes** <note>original: "Sepulchrum Hermetis"</note>, and more of the same. # Fourth Chapter.

...and when a word in one language, which implies two things at once, provides a great advantage (if it was chosen with diligence), yet in the translation into another language it refers to only one of those things—and if it happens to be the less significant one—this is once again a disadvantage for learners. What shall I say of those who falsely imagine they understand something proper of this **science** <term>Wissenschaft: in this context, the "sacred science" of alchemy</term>, and in translation presume to improve the **author** <note>original: "Autorem"</note> in one thing or another, and change whole words? But I tell you, my children, that all their words must be well considered; for all of them, without exception, are placed with great deliberation, and many a single word can provide you with such light that through it you may grasp the entire meaning and context.

Therefore, read the **philosophers** <term>Philosophos: the master alchemists and authors of Hermetic texts</term> very thoughtfully, with reflection and application toward **nature’s possibility** <note>that is, what is actually possible within the laws of the natural world, as opposed to "sophistical" or impossible recipes</note>, for as long and as much as it takes until you have achieved a good general concept of the entire work and its successive **operations** <term>Operationen: the specific laboratory procedures or spiritual steps in the alchemical process</term>. In doing so, you should not fail to make **extractions** <note>original: "Extractiones"; here meaning study notes, summaries, or excerpts from the text</note> for yourselves, and to draft, as it were, a plan according to which you intend to manage your affair; yet do so in such a way that you call the **matter** <note>original: "Materiam"; the starting substance of the Work</note> by a **mystical name**, or designate it with a **symbol** <term>Caracter: a shorthand alchemical sign or sigil used to hide secrets</term>, so that if your plan be true, it is safe from others; and if not, you will not be laughed at by someone into whose hands your notes might fall.

->On the Reading of Philosophical-Hermetic Works, etc.<-

D 4

Finally, I give you this advice, from which you will draw no small benefit: that, once you have gained a fairly good understanding of the **philosophical books** <term>philosophischen Büchern: referring to the works of the "philosophers" or master alchemists</term>, you choose a whole day for yourself now and then, in which you read one or another philosopher thoughtfully from beginning to end, in order to form a proper idea of the whole work in this way. For only in this way can you recognize whether such an author has described the work in its proper order—which few have done—or if he begins in the middle and provides the beginning at the end, or whether he presents the beginning and the end one after another, but leaves the middle for the last, and so forth, as **Trevisanus** <note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous Italian alchemist known for his arduous search for the Philosopher's Stone</note> does in the fourth part of his **Practice** <note>original: "Practic"</note>. Once you obtain this advantage of recognizing such things, there is no doubt that you will also reach a perfect understanding of the entire work and be **successful** <note>original: "glücklich"; in alchemical texts, this often refers to the successful completion of the Great Work</note>.

If, through further reading of these books, it then seems you can receive no more light, or if you believe you have enough, then I advise you to choose one or at most two of them, with which you can then remain. Among these, I recommend to you the *Philosophical Father-Heart* <note>Likely referring to a specific alchemical treatise</note>, but especially the *Hermetic Triumph* <note>Referring to "Le Triomphe Hermétique" (1689) by Limojon de Saint-Didier, one of the most highly regarded alchemical texts of the 17th century</note>, as the latter is a jewel and the crown of all **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophorum"</note>; even its **hieroglyph** <term>Hieroglyph: a symbolic or emblematic image used in alchemy to represent complex chemical and spiritual processes</term> is already clearer than any found among so many hundreds; moreover, in his little book, he says more than any Philo-

# Chapter Four.

...soph has not said in the largest folio. You may believe me in this. Therefore, it is also rightly titled ***The Hermetic Triumph*** <note>original: "Hermetische Triumph"; a famous alchemical treatise first published in 1701, attributed to Limojon de Saint-Didier</note>, not, as the author says, simply because it is appropriate since the **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note> gains victory over **Gold**—for the author gives himself too little credit—but because it simultaneously achieves victory over all philosophers who have ever written, through its incomparable, sincere work that reveals the truth without much pomp.

However, so that you may also be certain of your sufficient reading and the knowledge obtained from it, it is necessary that you have a proof within yourself, which you can recognize by this: namely, if you can clearly understand the entire work from just a few words cited here and there by the philosophers. For example, if one were to say: our **art** is essentially nothing but the **union of the upper and lower waters** <note>A fundamental alchemical concept from the Emerald Tablet, referring to the reconciliation of the volatile (spiritual) and fixed (physical) aspects of matter</note>; this is saying both little and much for one who already has a perfect understanding of the work. Or as the philosopher **Geber** <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–c. 815), often considered the father of chemistry</note> says: that our **stone** is nothing other than a **stinking spirit** <term>stinkender Geist: a common term for crude, unrefined sulfur or the "Black Sun" stage of the process</term>, and a **living water**, which we then called a **dry water** <term>trockenes Wasser: an alchemical paradox usually referring to mercurial substances that behave like liquid but do not wet surfaces</term>, purified through a natural proportion and joined with such a union that one cannot exist without the other; to this, one should also add the **Third** <note>Likely the "Secret Fire" or the salt that mediates between the spirit and the body</note>, which shortens the work. Also...

## Of the Reading of Philosophical-Hermetic [Works], etc.

...er <note>Completing the phrase "dry water" from the previous page</note> at another place: that one cleanses the **stone** through the way of **sublimation** in the most perfect manner, and thereafter with rational skill confirms the volatile from them, and afterwards that the constant becomes volatile, and the volatile becomes constant in the fire. Or even shorter: that one **coagulates** <note>solidifies</note> the purified stone, then again twice **dissolves** and **coagulates** in its own blood or **sulfur**.

The incomparable **Sendivogius** <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a Polish alchemist famous for his theory that "life-food" (oxygen/nitre) exists in the air</note> explains it in such a manner several times in the conclusion of his *Twelve Treatises*, and at the end of the twelfth: namely, **dissolve** a **body** <term>Corpus: the physical metal or substance being worked upon</term>, whichever one you wish, and whatever has come to it from nature of a different kind <note>i.e., impurities or "accidental" qualities</note>, separate that from it; purify it, and put pure and pure, cooked and cooked, raw and raw together, according to the weight of nature, and not of the matter <note>Alchemical "weight" often refers to the internal "virtue" or strength of a substance rather than its literal weight on a scale</note>. Likewise: Take <note>original: "℞", the medical/alchemical symbol for Recipe</note> 10 parts of the **air**, 1 part **living gold or silver**, put all of this into a vessel, first cook this air so that it becomes water, and afterwards not water. Likewise: that you dissolve the congealed air, and in the same dissolve the tenth part of gold, seal this and work with our fire, until the air becomes powder; and when you have the **salt of the world**, many colors will appear, etc. Likewise, even more clearly: that it is nothing other than our **Pontic water** <note>original: "pontisch Wasser"; a specific mercurial solvent used in the "Great Work"</note>, which is congealed in the sun and moon, and is drawn out of the sun and moon with our **Chalybs** <note>original: "Chalybe"; from the Greek word for steel. In alchemy, this refers to the "Magnet of the Sages" used to attract the celestial influence from the atmosphere</note>.

### Fourth Chapter.

Basilius Valentinus <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist, purportedly a Benedictine monk, though his writings likely date to the late 16th century</note> says it in this way in his fifth key: our **matter** <term>Materia: the base substance or "First Matter" chosen for the Great Work</term> must, at the beginning of our work, be well and most highly purified, then unlocked and broken apart; when all of this has happened, then prepare from it a **flying spirit** <note>original: "fliegenden Geist"; a volatile substance or vapor that rises during distillation</note>, white as the snow, and another flying spirit, red as blood. Join these two spirits together, which preserve and increase life; give them what they naturally require of food and drink, and keep them in the **marriage-bed of heat** <note>A metaphorical term for the alchemical furnace or flask where substances are combined under constant, gentle temperature</note> until the perfect birth. And many others speak in such a manner.

Do you now understand them perfectly well, so that you see the whole Art within it? Good! If not, I advise you once more: read and study and work with your head until no doubt remains and you no longer face the slightest hesitation; for I tell you truly that this Art cannot be obtained through mere trial and error if you do not already know in advance what you wish to create or produce. 

In addition to this, for a better understanding of the **philosophical writings** <note>The standard term for alchemical texts, written by "philosophers" of nature</note>, I advise you that you should also draw everything else toward them and apply it to them, so that you see and hear nothing without seeking to draw some benefit from it by inquiring into the cause. For example: you hear many common proverbs which nevertheless do not fail to indicate high matters at their core, such as: *the early hour has gold in its mouth* <note>original: "Früh-Stund hat Gold im Mund"; while similar to "the early bird catches the worm," in alchemy this often refers to the "Golden" influence of the morning sun or the collection of morning dew, a prized solvent</note>; the love of the—

# On the Reading of Philosophical-Hermetic Works, etc.

Next, one begins with oneself; one does not approach such a matter with unwashed hands; nothing is good for the eyes; he who carries everything with him needs nothing foreign; he who is not silent has no secret; pearls do not belong to swine; to know oneself is the greatest science; no one will spoon-feed you <note>original: "man wird ihm das Koch nicht in das Maul streichen"; a German idiom meaning information will not be made effortlessly easy or "chewed" for the reader</note> and similar things that you will encounter here and there in the **philosophical books** <note>Meaning alchemical texts written by "philosophers of nature"</note>. Thus you also see many beautiful buildings, which you should not only look at and admire and let it be; but rather consider this or that piece very well.

As, for example, the various **vessels** <term>Vasa: Latin for containers or laboratory vessels, here being compared to architectural ornaments</term> found upon those large buildings: why are some of them round and low, and even surrounded with **ram and bull heads**? <note>These represent the zodiacal signs of Aries and Taurus, signifying the spring season when the "Universal Spirit" is said to be most potent</note> Others are somewhat high and too narrow at the top, where flames of fire or flowers emerge? These vessels are remnants of the old style of building, along with the poetic figures and deities; all of which can provide you no small advantage for your work.

Especially if you can obtain drawings of the ancient temples, which contain much that is useful; as then the **Ark of the Covenant** <note>original: "Arche des Bunds"; the sacred chest described in the Book of Exodus</note> (e) along with its vessels, such as bowls for liquid sacrifice, the seven-branched lampstand, the vessel with the **showbread** <note>Sacred bread placed in the Temple; here interpreted as an alchemical symbol for the "Stone" or the "Bread of Life"</note>, (p) and others, are full of mystery. ## Chapter Five.

### On the Primary Matter, or more properly the Secondary, with which the Artist begins the Work.

<margin>M. I.</margin>
The **Primary Matter** <term>Materia Prima: The fundamental, formless substance from which all things are made; the "first stuff" of creation</term> is commonly called that with which the artist begins the work, but improperly so. For the **Primary Matter** is of a diaphanous <note>Transparent or translucent</note>, pure essence—a pure spirit (υ), light, or life. Although this light is found in all sublunary <note>Sublunary: literally "below the moon," referring to the changing, earthly realm as opposed to the unchanging heavens</note> things, (φ) in most it is present in very small amounts; in others more, but in one single being it is present most of all. This is the very **Subject** <note>The specific physical substance or "starting material" chosen by the alchemist</note> which the **philosophers** <note>Here meaning alchemists or seekers of natural wisdom</note> take for their work. From this, as the **Secondary Matter** <term>Materia Secunda: The physical, tangible substance used in the lab, which acts as a container for the spiritual Primary Matter</term>, the Primary or universal matter <note>original: "Materia Prima seu universalis"</note> is first extracted.

However, so that you, my children, may learn to understand this better, you must know: that in all individual things, there is something more hidden than merely the four **elements**; namely, the aforementioned spirit or light, which, like a seed in its

# The Fifth Chapter.

housing, the fire has its resting place or residence, and it may be called the **universal seed** <term>Universal-Saamen: A primal creative spark or "world-soul" believed to be the source of all life and growth</term> of the whole world; it enlivens, preserves, and consumes everything, and the more this is found in a thing of the three kingdoms <note>The three kingdoms of nature: animal, vegetable, and mineral</note>, the nobler that thing is above others.

This light flows down from above from the **fountain of light** <note>original: "Licht-Brunnen"</note>, namely the sun, abundantly—and it flows the more abundantly the closer the sun comes to our pole <note>Referring to the changing of seasons and the sun's proximity to the northern hemisphere</note>—down onto and into the earth, penetrating within, and imparting its light to all individual things of the subterranean realm: to some more and some less, according to what the **vessel** <term>Receptacul: From the Latin 'receptaculum,' meaning a container or physical body capable of receiving and holding spiritual essence</term> is able to grasp and retain within itself. Of these, **gold** possesses the very most among all other minerals and metals, as indicated by both its luster and its permanence. For this reason, it is also called the noblest of the mineral kingdom and the king of metals.

Upon the earth, all **plants** <term>Vegetabilia: The vegetable or plant kingdom</term> gather this light into themselves in great quantities; and according to how the roots—serving as their vessels—are stronger and more impregnated with a **fixed salt** <term>Sale fixo: A stable, chemical salt that does not evaporate or "fly away" when heated, acting as a physical anchor for spiritual energy</term>, the more they draw of such light to themselves. Some shrubs and trees even release the excess through their bark as a thick sap, which is hardened by the air into a gum or resin. This is full of light or **sulfur** <note>In alchemical theory, sulfur represents the active, flammable principle and the "soul" of a substance</note>, as can be seen in frankincense, myrrh, and others by the flame and scent when they are ignited. However, of all these, **wine** possesses the very most, and not

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "universal light" or "spirit" and how it manifests across the three kingdoms of nature (mineral, vegetable, and animal), specifically focusing on how plants and animals concentrate this celestial essence.</meta>

-> **On the First Matter, or more precisely, etc.** <-

...not only what comes down daily from above, but also what rises back up from the earth into the heights and unites there, so that, so to speak, the **vegetable kingdom** <term>Vegetabilische: The realm of plants and vegetation</term> participates in something of the mineral. I once saw this with my own eyes when I traveled to **Upper Hungary** <note>A historical region of the Kingdom of Hungary, mostly corresponding to modern-day Slovakia and parts of Northern Hungary</note> near **Tokaj** in the wine-growing hills of Mada, Tarzal, and Tallya. There, a host showed me a grapevine cut in two, in which subtle gold threads could be seen running lengthwise, as fine as a spinner can spin a thread; there were also seeds that were speckled with gold. From this, it is clearly evident that this **liquid light** or **volatile gold** <note>original: "flüchtigen Goldes"</note> was drawn up by the grapevine through the roots and **fixed** <term>Figiret: From the Latin "fixare," an alchemical term for the process of turning a "volatile" or airy substance into a solid, stable form through heat</term> within it by the heat of the sun. From this, one can also conclude the excellence of Tokaj wine; and if wine is called a king in the vegetable kingdom, this one is doubly worthy of the title.

In the **Animal Kingdom** <term>Animalischen Reich: The realm of living, breathing creatures</term>, everything that has life breathes and draws such light into itself; but since, in addition to this, animals nourish themselves upon plants, they are double partakers of this light. Consequently, the lowliest animal is nobler than the noblest member of the vegetable kingdom.

Since, however, **Man** not only breathes, but also nourishes himself upon both animals and plants at once, he is a triple partaker of this light; and thus the lowliest of men is nobler than all animals.

### Chapter Five.
<meta>catchword: concerns-</meta>

...er <note>This fragment completes a word from the previous page, likely 'dicker' (plumper) or 'frischer' (fresher), referring to the healthy appearance of mothers.</note>; not just from the bearing of children itself, but because they strengthen their spirit from the spirit of the husband, which then, through food and drink, builds for itself a much stronger and more perfect body than it was previously able to do; as one clearly sees the difference between a maiden and a woman, and as more has already been said regarding **procreation** <term>procreation: original 'Generation', referring here to the biological and spiritual process of conception and development</term> in the second chapter. Of those who utterly abuse nature, and even reject it; I will not, nor am I permitted to, even mention them. O you great world, worrying too much and so falsely! How much happier, I say once more, are those among the lowly; whom you nonetheless laugh at in your heart. Ah! May God most graciously steer us away from this.

Let no one take it ill of me that I have strayed so far from my **aim** <term>aim: original 'Scopo' (from the Latin 'scopus'), meaning the target or objective of the argument</term>; I have once begun writing, and for your sake—you who do not approve of my order—I will not restrain my thoughts simply because this does not strictly belong to the matter: for you must know that I write from no self-interest, and consequently, I let it flow just as it comes to my pen. Admittedly, at the beginning of the chapters, I intended nothing less than to mix in such matters; nevertheless, it is good and true, and I am just as entitled to teach this or that, for I have an equal share of both. Meanwhile, you must attribute it to my zeal, which I have for **truth** and **justice**. And since it concerns the common good and **population** <note>original: 'Bevölkerung'; the author links the health and morality of mothers to the demographic growth and strength of society</note>...

...concerns <note>This completes the word "betrifft" from the previous page.</note>, I wish that someone whose business it is—for it is not my place—would write about it and bring something to light that could go even further. Furthermore, they should imagine what incomparable people they would become if the tender offspring of such high-born individuals also received their first nourishment from the noble juice <note>original: "edlen Saft"; the author refers to breast milk, which was historically believed to be a form of "whitened" maternal blood carrying the mother's character and nobility.</note> of their own mothers. 

Certainly! Just as nature always strives for something higher and more perfect, I say certainly that the children of such high-ranking persons would become even more beautiful, tender, noble, and refined of mind—in a word, they would soon become like earthly angels. On the other hand, such a writer would also have to point out the misfortune that arises from the idle breasts of such mothers (whose internal milk ducts then grow together and atrophy), which causes no small detriment to the human race. From such mothers, daughters with inferior breasts are born, who in turn give birth to those even worse, and so the noble lineage is stunted <note>original: "verstümmelt," meaning mutilated or crippled. # Chapter Five

...to open <note>This continues the thought from the previous page regarding the "opening" of the internal milk ducts through breastfeeding.</note>: for while on the one hand they fear sharing the excess fluid <note>original: "Saft"; here referring to breast milk</note> with their children, on the other hand, they squander the necessary fluid through so much bloodletting throughout the year so recklessly that it is truly irresponsible—especially for those who order it and should know better. As for the others, they are not so much to be blamed, as they allow themselves to be swept away by this creeping abuse of the practice. 

How easy it would be for me, through God, Nature, and Scripture, to show that it is not at all necessary to let anyone’s blood. God has already built man in such a way that, in the event of an oncoming illness, Nature can properly discharge a certain quantity of blood through one place or another. This is naturally ordained for the female sex <note>A reference to menstruation.</note>, and also occurs from time to time in some men through nosebleeds, the golden vein <term>golden vein: "goldene Ader," a historical medical term for hemorrhoids, which were often viewed as a natural and healthy way for the male body to expel "excess" blood</term>, and the like. 

Furthermore, thousands of ages <note>original Latin: "Sæcula"</note> have passed during which people were subject to all the same diseases as we are today, and were likewise freed from them, yet they never practiced bloodletting. They were, in fact, satisfied to see themselves helped by the natural "bloodlettings" of insensible perspiration <note>original: "unempfindliche Ausdünstung"; a historical medical theory regarding the invisible release of vapors through the skin</note>, sweat, urine, bowel movements, discharge, and such—all of which are also essentially "blood" <note>In the humoral medicine of the time, all bodily fluids were seen as related or derived from the same vital source.</note>. Has not Moses already taught us that the spirit or the life exists in the blood? Now, the more or the stronger the life is, the more powerfully it can resist disease or...

# The Fifth Chapter.

->### Concerning the First Matter, or more properly, etc.<-

I have previously shown you how the <term>First Matter <note>original: "prima Materia"; the primordial, formless substance from which all elements were thought to originate</note></term> is present in all things of the three kingdoms <note>The "three kingdoms of nature" are the Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral kingdoms</note>, originating from the upper or fourth kingdom; likewise, I have shown in which of these it is most abundantly found. Now I must warn you to be very careful in your search for it, and to remember that the philosophers teach that their matter must not yet be "specified"—meaning you should not choose a specific metal, herb, or animal—but rather it should still be in a <term>universal state <note>original: "Statu universali"</note></term>. In this state, it possesses the qualities of all three kingdoms, yet cannot be categorized as any one in particular; it is a subject where Nature has indeed begun her work, but has left it lying incomplete. Therefore, you may also be spared from seeking to obtain it from the elements or <term>ethereal essences <note>original: "Æthærischen Wesen"</note></term>—such as hail, dew, rain, or the air and sunbeams themselves—by means of many types of machines, glasses, mirrors, and the like; for a human lifespan would not be sufficient for such a task.

Whatever matter you may read about in the writings of the philosophers, accept it naturally and philosophically—that is, only insofar as they indicate the "right" matter and how they wish to proceed with it, not that the substance they name is the matter itself. To see that this is so, you need only remember what they themselves say: namely, that out of several hundred philosophers, not one has ever called it by its plain name, except

# Concerning the First Matter, or more properly, etc.

...[take] two or three, and not in the place where one usually seeks them.

3 E. Thus, when they specify <term>Antimony <note>original: "Antimonium"</note></term> as their matter, they only intend to indicate the qualities of their matter by it. Just as Antimony is an immature mineral—a "mountain-vapor" <note>original: "Berg-Schwaden"; volatile mineral gases or fumes found in mines, often considered "unripe" precursors to metals</note> that does not truly resemble any specific metal, even though it possesses the <term>principles <note>original: "Principia"; the fundamental components (often Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury) believed to make up all matter</note></term> of all other metals together—so too is their matter a substance of mineral and metallic natures joined together, though it is not a mineral or metal itself. Furthermore, just as Antimony contains all colors within it and is volatile, so too can all colors be discovered within their matter, which is likewise volatile; for this reason, they name it "all in one or one in all," or Antimony <note>original: "omnia in uno seu unum in omnia vel Antimonium"</note>.

If they call it <term>Vitriol <note>original: "Vitriolum"; a term for sulfate minerals, usually highly colorful and reactive</note></term>, they wish it to be understood in the same manner as previously stated. For just as Vitriol is actually a coagulated metallic water or salt that can easily be dissolved again by water, so too is their matter of a similar nature. For their "Vitriol" is truly formed from a heavenly and earthly water combined into an earth or salt. With this earth, they can bring forth all colors, just as occurs in common Vitriol through <term>calcination <note>original: "calciniren"; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off water or change its chemical state</note></term>. However, when they say that their Vitriol is not just any kind, but that which comes from Hungary, then...

<summary>This page explains that alchemical terms like "Antimony" and "Vitriol" are often metaphors for the First Matter's properties—its volatility, its contains-all-colors nature, and its origin from "heavenly and earthly water"—rather than the literal substances.</summary>

...should also be understood concerning <term>Antimony <note>original: "Antimonio"</note></term>. From this name <note>Referring to the name VITRIOL</note> comes the maxim: **Visit the interior of the earth; by rectifying, you will find the hidden stone and the true medicine.** <note>original Latin: "Visita Interiora Terræ Rectificando Invenies Occultum Lapidem Veramque Medicinam." The first letters of each word in this Latin phrase form the acronym V.I.T.R.I.O.L., a famous alchemical motto.</note> This phrase simultaneously describes the entire alchemical process.

If they specify <term>Gold</term> or <term>Mercury <note>original: "Quecksilber"</note></term> as their matter, and say at the same time that it is not the "common" kind, but that they first produced their "philosophical" or "living" Gold and <term>Quick-silver <note>original: "Argentum vivum"</note></term> from it through <term>opening <note>original: "Aufschliessung"; the chemical process of breaking a substance down to its primary components</note></term> and reversal—then this "common" gold and mercury are still not the well-known metallic gold and mercury that come from the mines. Instead, they call their as-yet unworked matter "common gold and mercury." See from what kind of dream and error I am rescuing you! Yet few will believe it nonetheless; for a short time ago, I [heard/witnessed] one of two otherwise worthy men [doing/saying] this...
